People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. I do get scared when I freeze up or feel confused, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like a panic thing.\n\n[Last Question]\nDid these episodes of freezing up and confusion at work seem to be triggered by something specific happening or was it more random?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Disorganized_Abnormal_Behavior\nmanifestation: \"What scared everyone at work is that I started freezing up in the middle of service or doing things out of order, like standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and repeating table numbers instead of closing checks.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I mean, I get worried after things happen, and I’ll replay it a bit, but I don’t have one specific thought that keeps forcing its way in or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t have rituals or things I feel like I have to do to make anxiety go away. I might get tense or confused, but I’m not doing specific routines on purpose like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them episodes like that. I do get scared when I freeze up or feel confused, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like a panic thing.\n\nDoctor: Did these episodes of freezing up and confusion at work seem to be triggered by something specific happening or was it more random?\n\nPatient: It seemed more random to me. Like, it would happen right in the middle of service, not always because of one specific thing. I’d just freeze up or start doing things out of order—like standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and repeating table numbers instead of closing checks. That’s what scared everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events recently or in the past that might be connected to what's been happening at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Nothing traumatic really comes to mind, either recently or from before, that I can connect to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain tasks at work since these episodes started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been clearly avoiding work on purpose, but I do feel uneasy about it now. I still try to do what I’m supposed to, though I’m not sure if I’m steering away from certain tasks without realizing it.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been affecting your relationships with coworkers or your overall ability to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s gotten bad enough that I think I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity. My mistakes and the confusion have messed up service and closing more than once, and people notice.\n\nAnd at home too… my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t mean to be like that, but I guess I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really do the things I used to do much anymore. I used to go for walks on the beach, bake sometimes, work in the garden, write in my journal… but lately I just don’t get myself to start any of it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. It’s not that I’m trying to ignore him. It just feels like I can’t really care or respond the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms at work are related to any particular stress or problem you're experiencing outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t point to one specific problem outside of work that set it off. Things at home have been strained, but I don’t know if that’s causing the work stuff or if it’s just all mixed together.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. It’s more that I get confused or kind of stuck, but not in that way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you feel like you've been feeling less emotional or caring about things that normally would be important to you?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 32 / waiter_or_waitress / some_college\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Negative_Symptoms\nmanifestation: \"I don’t really go for beach walks, bake, garden, or write in my journal much anymore, and when my husband tries to talk with me I mostly just sit there because it feels like I can’t get myself to care or start anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Plain patient.\nA plain patient describes their symptoms and experiences in a direct and straightforward manner, providing clear and relevant answers to the doctor's questions.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

Yeah… I think so. I don’t really do the things I used to care about, like walking on the beach, baking, gardening, or writing in my journal. I just kind of stopped.

Nothing traumatic really comes to mind, either recently or from before, that I can connect to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain tasks at work since these episodes started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been clearly avoiding work on purpose, but I do feel uneasy about it now. I still try to do what I’m supposed to, though I’m not sure if I’m steering away from certain tasks without realizing it.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been affecting your relationships with coworkers or your overall ability to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s gotten bad enough that I think I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity. My mistakes and the confusion have messed up service and closing more than once, and people notice.\n\nAnd at home too… my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t mean to be like that, but I guess I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really do the things I used to do much anymore. I used to go for walks on the beach, bake sometimes, work in the garden, write in my journal… but lately I just don’t get myself to start any of it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. It’s not that I’m trying to ignore him. It just feels like I can’t really care or respond the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms at work are related to any particular stress or problem you're experiencing outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t point to one specific problem outside of work that set it off. Things at home have been strained, but I don’t know if that’s causing the work stuff or if it’s just all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy life or function normally?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not just bothering me, it’s getting in the way of normal things. At work I’m making mistakes and getting confused enough that service and closing have been disrupted, and I’m worried I’m going to lose the shift-lead opportunity.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t want that, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m outside my body or like I’m dreaming. It’s more that I get confused or kind of stuck, but not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been feeling less emotional or caring about things that normally would be important to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really do the things I used to care about, like walking on the beach, baking, gardening, or writing in my journal. I just kind of stopped.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. It’s not that I want to ignore him, it just feels like I can’t get myself to care or even start responding the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience emotions the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like the feeling is dulled down. I don’t really get excited to do things I used to like, like beach walks or baking or gardening, and I barely write in my journal anymore.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. I know I should care and respond, but it feels like I can’t really start, or I can’t get myself to feel much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy more than you feel like you're just not able to experience emotions about them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I’ve lost interest, but also I just can’t get myself to start anything. I don’t really go for beach walks or bake or garden anymore, and I hardly write in my journal.\n\nEven when my husband tries to talk with me, I mostly just sit there. It feels like I can’t make myself care enough to respond.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF43.0, F32.0, F33.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things got scary at work. I’m a waitress, and during service I started freezing up, like just stopping in the middle of what I was doing. Sometimes I’d do things completely out of order, like stand by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and just keep repeating table numbers instead of closing checks. It upset people, and honestly it scared me too.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any recurring, intrusive thoughts that you can't seem to get out of your head?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I get worried after things happen, and I’ll replay it a bit, but I don’t have one specific thought that keeps forcing its way in or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t have rituals or things I feel like I have to do to make anxiety go away. I might get tense or confused, but I’m not doing specific routines on purpose like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them episodes like that. I do get scared when I freeze up or feel confused, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like a panic thing.\n\nDoctor: Did these episodes of freezing up and confusion at work seem to be triggered by something specific happening or was it more random?\n\nPatient: It seemed more random to me. Like, it would happen right in the middle of service, not always because of one specific thing. I’d just freeze up or start doing things out of order—like standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and repeating table numbers instead of closing checks. That’s what scared everyone.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any traumatic events recently or in the past that might be connected to what's been happening at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. Nothing traumatic really comes to mind, either recently or from before, that I can connect to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain tasks at work since these episodes started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been clearly avoiding work on purpose, but I do feel uneasy about it now. I still try to do what I’m supposed to, though I’m not sure if I’m steering away from certain tasks without realizing it.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been affecting your relationships with coworkers or your overall ability to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s gotten bad enough that I think I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity. My mistakes and the confusion have messed up service and closing more than once, and people notice.\n\nAnd at home too… my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t mean to be like that, but I guess I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really do the things I used to do much anymore. I used to go for walks on the beach, bake sometimes, work in the garden, write in my journal… but lately I just don’t get myself to start any of it.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. It’s not that I’m trying to ignore him. It just feels like I can’t really care or respond the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms at work are related to any particular stress or problem you're experiencing outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t point to one specific problem outside of work that set it off. Things at home have been strained, but I don’t know if that’s causing the work stuff or if it’s just all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy life or function normally?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not just bothering me, it’s getting in the way of normal things. At work I’m making mistakes and getting confused enough that service and closing have been disrupted, and I’m worried I’m going to lose the shift-lead opportunity.\n\nAnd at home, my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t want that, but that’s how it’s been.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're observing yourself from outside your body or like you're in a dream when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I haven’t really noticed feeling like I’m outside my body or like I’m dreaming. It’s more that I get confused or kind of stuck, but not in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've been feeling less emotional or caring about things that normally would be important to you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t really do the things I used to care about, like walking on the beach, baking, gardening, or writing in my journal. I just kind of stopped.\n\nAnd when my husband tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. It’s not that I want to ignore him, it just feels like I can’t get myself to care or even start responding the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to experience emotions the way you used to?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s like the feeling is dulled down. I don’t really get excited to do things I used to like, like beach walks or baking or gardening, and I barely write in my journal anymore.\n\nAnd with my husband, when he tries to talk to me, I mostly just sit there. I know I should care and respond, but it feels like I can’t really start, or I can’t get myself to feel much.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in things you used to enjoy more than you feel like you're just not able to experience emotions about them?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more like I’ve lost interest, but also I just can’t get myself to start anything. It’s been enough that my coworkers noticed.\n\nDoctor: How many weeks or months have you been feeling this way, with the loss of interest and emotional dulling?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been building for about a year and two months now. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I came in because things got scary at work. I’m a waitress, and during service I started freezing up, like just stopping in the middle of what I was doing. I mean, I get worried after things happen, and I’ll replay it a bit, but I don’t have one specific thought that keeps forcing its way in or anything like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel compelled to perform specific rituals or behaviors to relieve anxiety or stress?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I don’t have rituals or things I feel like I have to do to make anxiety go away. I might get tense or confused, but I’m not doing specific routines on purpose like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reached a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call them episodes like that. I do get scared when I freeze up or feel confused, but I haven’t noticed it coming on suddenly and peaking within a few minutes like a panic thing.\n\nDoctor: Did these episodes of freezing up and confusion at work seem to be triggered by something specific happening or was it more random?\n\nPatient: It seemed more random to me. Like, it would happen right in the middle of service, not always because of one specific thing. I’d just freeze up or start doing things out of order—like standing by the soda station holding the cash drawer key and repeating table numbers instead of closing checks. Nothing traumatic really comes to mind, either recently or from before, that I can connect to what’s been happening at work.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding going to work or certain tasks at work since these episodes started happening?\n\nPatient: I don’t really know. I haven’t been clearly avoiding work on purpose, but I do feel uneasy about it now. I still try to do what I’m supposed to, though I’m not sure if I’m steering away from certain tasks without realizing it.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been affecting your relationships with coworkers or your overall ability to function at work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s gotten bad enough that I think I’m close to losing the shift-lead opportunity. My mistakes and the confusion have messed up service and closing more than once, and people notice.\n\nAnd at home too… my husband says I’m barely present with him anymore. I don’t mean to be like that, but I guess I’m not really there the way I used to be.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your relationships or daily activities outside of work due to how you've been feeling lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I don’t really do the things I used to do much anymore. It’s not that I’m trying to ignore him. It just feels like I can’t really care or respond the way I should.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms at work are related to any particular stress or problem you're experiencing outside of work?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I can’t point to one specific problem outside of work that set it off. Things at home have been strained, but I don’t know if that’s causing the work stuff or if it’s just all mixed together.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your symptoms are significantly interfering with your ability to enjoy life or function normally?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it’s not just bothering me, it’s getting in the way of normal things. It’s like the feeling is dulled down. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, not more than two years. It’s been about a year and two months, since around when I was asked to take on the evening shift lead responsibilities.
